[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is… I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—especially when I have to go into town or deal with bankers or county people, I feel like they’re all staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors his whole life, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face and hands, yeah. I mean, those are the parts people see first, especially when I’m sitting across from someone at the bank or talking to county folks about paperwork. I start thinking they’re looking at the cracks in my skin, the spots, the way my face seems to sag now.\n\nMy wife tells me it’s just what happens when a man’s worked outdoors his whole life, you know, sun and weather and machinery grease and all that, but… I don’t know. It doesn’t feel normal to me when I’m out around people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face and hands most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yeah… I guess so. I don’t always notice it right away, like if I’m out fixing something or dealing with the fields, but as soon as I’ve got to see people—bankers, county office folks, anybody like that—it gets in my head.\n\nI keep thinking about how rough my face looks, how worn my hands are, the cracks and spots and all that sagging. My wife says it’s just from working outside all these years, and maybe she’s right, but it’s hard for me to let it go. It kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the appearance of your face and hands interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. More than I like to admit. I’ve let calls from bankers go because I couldn’t get myself feeling like I looked decent enough to sit down and talk things through, even just on the phone knowing I’d have to meet them later. And then that holds up farm decisions, paperwork, things that ought to just get done.\n\nAnd with the retreat and the craft sales—my wife’s always saying a picture helps people feel welcome, or helps sell what we’re doing, and I know she’s right. But I’ve stopped agreeing to be in photos. I’ll find some excuse about needing to check a fence or move equipment, but really it’s because I don’t want my face or hands out there for people to look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands are actually abnormal in their appearance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “abnormal” is the right word… I mean, my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outside his whole life, and she’s probably trying to be kind, but when I look at myself, I can’t get past how rough and worn everything looks.\n\nMy face, my hands… the cracks, spots, the sagging. And then if I’m sitting across from a banker or somebody at the county office, I feel like that’s all they’re seeing. Like they’re staring at it, even if maybe they aren’t. I know plenty of men my age have weathered skin, but mine feels… worse somehow. Hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: When your wife or others tell you that you look fine, that your skin is just a result of working outdoors, do you think they're seeing the same things you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they are, no… or maybe they see it but it doesn’t hit them the same way. My wife will say, “That’s just sun and work,” like it’s nothing, like the old barn siding getting weathered—actually, we had boards on the south side go gray just like that after a few seasons—but with me, I can’t just see it as normal wear.\n\nWhen I’m looking at my face or my hands, all I can see are the cracks and spots and the sagging. And then if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I get it in my head that they’re noticing every bit of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the appearance of your face and hands stay the same all the time, or are there moments when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same every minute, no. If I’m out working and I’ve got gloves on, or I’m focused on some repair, I can forget it for a bit. Like when you’re fixing a gate hinge and the bolt’s stripped, you’re not thinking about much else.\n\nBut then if I have to go into town, or sit down with a banker or somebody from the county office, it comes right back. I start thinking they’re looking at my face, my hands, the cracks and spots and sagging. Even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how your face and hands look ever change, or is it always the same in your mind?\n\nPatient: It… it mostly stays the same in my mind, I guess. I mean, some days the light catches my face different or my hands are dirty from work, and I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just that, but I still come back to the same thing—that they look rough and worn out.\n\nIt’s the cracks and spots, the sagging, all of it. I’ll be sitting across from a banker or someone from the county office and I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that simple to me. It’s like once I notice it, my mind just locks onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt your face and hands looked better, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe once in a while, if I’ve just washed up and the light in the bathroom isn’t too harsh, I can think, well, maybe it’s not as bad right this second. But it doesn’t last.\n\nI keep coming back to how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… that’s what brings me in, really. I’ll be sitting with a banker or someone from the county, and it’s like I can feel them looking at every bit of it, even though my wife keeps saying I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nI know farmers’ hands get beat up—my dad’s did too, and he used to say you could tell a man’s work by his hands—but with me, I can’t leave it at that. I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trust, like your wife, were to tell you that you look fine and that your concerns about your appearance are unfounded, would that change how you feel about your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It might settle me for a minute, coming from her. I do trust my wife—she’s known me since before my hands got this beat up, back when we were renting land and trying to make a go of it. She’ll say I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she means it kindly.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t really change it for long. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… I’ll be sitting with a banker or somebody from the county office, and I’m sure they’re looking right at all of it. Even if she tells me it’s unfounded, my mind doesn’t seem to take that in the way it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face and hands actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's distorted?\n\nPatient: I suppose there’s a possibility, yes… I mean, my wife says that all the time, that I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. And I’ve seen plenty of men my age at the co-op with hands worse than mine, I guess.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m sitting across from a banker or someone at the county office. I can’t get past it—how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it’s like I’m sure they’re looking right at it. Even if part of me can say maybe it’s just my perception, the bigger part of me doesn’t believe it for long.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your appearance ever completely go away, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. I might get distracted for a short spell—if I’m fixing something or talking about crop prices or, you know, trying to remember whether I shut a gate—but it comes back.\n\nWhat brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… especially if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county. I start thinking they’re staring right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. It just doesn’t really leave me alone.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular aspect of your face or hands that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pick just one. I start with my hands, usually—the cracks and the rough skin, like they’ve been through too many seasons. But then I’ll catch my face in a window or under those office lights and I see the spots and the sagging, and then it’s all I can think about.\n\nI guess what bothers me most is the whole worn-out look of my face and hands together. Like when I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel sure they’re staring at it, sizing me up, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. I know she means well, but I can’t seem to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face and hands look normal to other people, even if they don't to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I know that’s what my wife says. She’ll tell me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and there are plenty of fellows around here who look weathered, you know, especially after years in the sun and dirt.\n\nBut when I’m sitting across from a banker, or somebody at the county office, I can’t shake it. I keep thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging… like it’s all right there and they can’t help but notice. So yes, maybe it looks normal to them, but it sure doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the cracks, spots, or sagging that bothers you, or is it more the overall look of your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It’s… more the overall look, I think. The cracks in my hands get me started, and then the spots on my face, the sagging—well, it all piles together until I just look rough and worn out to myself.\n\nLike if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county, I can’t help thinking they’re staring at all of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I suppose that ought to settle it, but it doesn’t. I keep seeing it, the whole weathered look, and I can’t get past it.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your face and hands looking rough and worn ever change in intensity from day to day?\n\nPatient: It does change some, yes. Some days I can get on with things a little better, especially if I’m busy and not sitting under bright lights or across from somebody important.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I can’t really get past it overall—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I’ll be talking to a banker or someone from the county and I start thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s trying to help, but once I notice it, it sort of takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice the cracks, spots, and sagging on your face and hands, or is it just you who thinks they might be staring at them?\n\nPatient: I think they notice it. Or… I guess I don’t know for sure, but it feels like they do. Especially if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county office, I get to thinking they’re looking right at my hands, the cracks, and then my face, the spots and the sagging.\n\nMy wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, same as plenty of men around here. And maybe she’s right. I mean, I’ve seen fellows at the feed store who look just as weathered, but somehow when it’s me, I can’t get past it. It feels like it’s written all over me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how your face and hands look ever change, or is it always the same in your mind?\n\nPatient: It… it mostly stays the same in my mind, I guess. I mean, some days the light catches my face different or my hands are dirty from work, and I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just that, but I still come back to the same thing—that they look rough and worn out.\n\nIt’s the cracks and spots, the sagging, all of it. I’ll be sitting across from a banker or someone from the county office and I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that simple to me. It’s like once I notice it, my mind just locks onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt your face and hands looked better, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe once in a while, if I’ve just washed up and the light in the bathroom isn’t too harsh, I can think, well, maybe it’s not as bad right this second. But it doesn’t last.\n\nI keep coming back to how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… that’s what brings me in, really. I’ll be sitting with a banker or someone from the county, and it’s like I can feel them looking at every bit of it, even though my wife keeps saying I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nI know farmers’ hands get beat up—my dad’s did too, and he used to say you could tell a man’s work by his hands—but with me, I can’t leave it at that. I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trust, like your wife, were to tell you that you look fine and that your concerns about your appearance are unfounded, would that change how you feel about your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It might settle me for a minute, coming from her. I do trust my wife—she’s known me since before my hands got this beat up, back when we were renting land and trying to make a go of it. She’ll say I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she means it kindly.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t really change it for long. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… I’ll be sitting with a banker or somebody from the county office, and I’m sure they’re looking right at all of it. Even if she tells me it’s unfounded, my mind doesn’t seem to take that in the way it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face and hands actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's distorted?\n\nPatient: I suppose there’s a possibility, yes… I mean, my wife says that all the time, that I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. And I’ve seen plenty of men my age at the co-op with hands worse than mine, I guess.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m sitting across from a banker or someone at the county office. I can’t get past it—how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it’s like I’m sure they’re looking right at it. Even if part of me can say maybe it’s just my perception, the bigger part of me doesn’t believe it for long.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your appearance ever completely go away, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. I might get distracted for a short spell—if I’m fixing something or talking about crop prices or, you know, trying to remember whether I shut a gate—but it comes back.\n\nWhat brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… especially if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county. I start thinking they’re staring right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. It just doesn’t really leave me alone.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular aspect of your face or hands that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pick just one. I start with my hands, usually—the cracks and the rough skin, like they’ve been through too many seasons. But then I’ll catch my face in a window or under those office lights and I see the spots and the sagging, and then it’s all I can think about.\n\nI guess what bothers me most is the whole worn-out look of my face and hands together. Like when I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel sure they’re staring at it, sizing me up, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. I know she means well, but I can’t seem to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face and hands look normal to other people, even if they don't to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I know that’s what my wife says. She’ll tell me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and there are plenty of fellows around here who look weathered, you know, especially after years in the sun and dirt.\n\nBut when I’m sitting across from a banker, or somebody at the county office, I can’t shake it. I keep thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging… like it’s all right there and they can’t help but notice. So yes, maybe it looks normal to them, but it sure doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the cracks, spots, or sagging that bothers you, or is it more the overall look of your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It’s… more the overall look, I think. The cracks in my hands get me started, and then the spots on my face, the sagging—well, it all piles together until I just look rough and worn out to myself.\n\nLike if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county, I can’t help thinking they’re staring at all of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I suppose that ought to settle it, but it doesn’t. I keep seeing it, the whole weathered look, and I can’t get past it.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your face and hands looking rough and worn ever change in intensity from day to day?\n\nPatient: It does change some, yes. Some days I can get on with things a little better, especially if I’m busy and not sitting under bright lights or across from somebody important.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I can’t really get past it overall—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I’ll be talking to a banker or someone from the county and I start thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s trying to help, but once I notice it, it sort of takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice the cracks, spots, and sagging on your face and hands, or is it just you who thinks they might be staring at them?\n\nPatient: I think they notice it. Or… I guess I don’t know for sure, but it feels like they do. Especially if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county office, I get to thinking they’re looking right at my hands, the cracks, and then my face, the spots and the sagging.\n\nMy wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, same as plenty of men around here. And maybe she’s right. I mean, I’ve seen fellows at the feed store who look just as weathered, but somehow when it’s me, I can’t get past it. It feels like it’s written all over me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But then I’ll catch my face in a window or under those office lights and I see the spots and the sagging, and then it’s all I can think about.\n\nI guess what bothers me most is the whole worn-out look of my face and hands together. Like when I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel sure they’re staring at it, sizing me up, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. I know she means well, but I can’t seem to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face and hands look normal to other people, even if they don't to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I know that’s what my wife says. She’ll tell me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and there are plenty of fellows around here who look weathered, you know, especially after years in the sun and dirt.\n\nBut when I’m sitting across from a banker, or somebody at the county office, I can’t shake it. I keep thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging… like it’s all right there and they can’t help but notice. So yes, maybe it looks normal to them, but it sure doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the cracks, spots, or sagging that bothers you, or is it more the overall look of your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It’s… more the overall look, I think. The cracks in my hands get me started, and then the spots on my face, the sagging—well, it all piles together until I just look rough and worn out to myself.\n\nLike if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county, I can’t help thinking they’re staring at all of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I suppose that ought to settle it, but it doesn’t. I keep seeing it, the whole weathered look, and I can’t get past it.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your face and hands looking rough and worn ever change in intensity from day to day?\n\nPatient: It does change some, yes. Some days I can get on with things a little better, especially if I’m busy and not sitting under bright lights or across from somebody important.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I can’t really get past it overall—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I’ll be talking to a banker or someone from the county and I start thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s trying to help, but once I notice it, it sort of takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice the cracks, spots, and sagging on your face and hands, or is it just you who thinks they might be staring at them?\n\nPatient: I think they notice it. Or… I guess I don’t know for sure, but it feels like they do. Especially if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county office, I get to thinking they’re looking right at my hands, the cracks, and then my face, the spots and the sagging.\n\nMy wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, same as plenty of men around here. And maybe she’s right. I mean, I’ve seen fellows at the feed store who look just as weathered, but somehow when it’s me, I can’t get past it. It feels like it’s written all over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face and hands because they are actually noticing something out of the ordinary, or because you feel self-conscious about how you look?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but it feels more like they’re noticing something. I don’t always notice it right away, like if I’m out fixing something or dealing with the fields, but as soon as I’ve got to see people—bankers, county office folks, anybody like that—it gets in my head.\n\nI keep thinking about how rough my face looks, how worn my hands are, the cracks and spots and all that sagging. My wife says it’s just from working outside all these years, and maybe she’s right, but it’s hard for me to let it go. It kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the appearance of your face and hands interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. More than I like to admit. I’ve let calls from bankers go because I couldn’t get myself feeling like I looked decent enough to sit down and talk things through, even just on the phone knowing I’d have to meet them later. But I’ve stopped agreeing to be in photos. I’ll find some excuse about needing to check a fence or move equipment, but really it’s because I don’t want my face or hands out there for people to look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands are actually abnormal in their appearance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “abnormal” is the right word… I mean, my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outside his whole life, and she’s probably trying to be kind, but when I look at myself, I can’t get past how rough and worn everything looks.\n\nMy face, my hands… the cracks, spots, the sagging. And then if I’m sitting across from a banker or somebody at the county office, I feel like that’s all they’re seeing. Like they’re staring at it, even if maybe they aren’t. My wife will say, “That’s just sun and work,” like it’s nothing, like the old barn siding getting weathered—actually, we had boards on the south side go gray just like that after a few seasons—but with me, I can’t just see it as normal wear.\n\nWhen I’m looking at my face or my hands, all I can see are the cracks and spots and the sagging. And then if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I get it in my head that they’re noticing every bit of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the appearance of your face and hands stay the same all the time, or are there moments when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same every minute, no. I mean, some days the light catches my face different or my hands are dirty from work, and I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just that, but I still come back to the same thing—that they look rough and worn out.\n\nIt’s the cracks and spots, the sagging, all of it. I’ll be sitting across from a banker or someone from the county office and I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that simple to me. It’s like once I notice it, my mind just locks onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt your face and hands looked better, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe once in a while, if I’ve just washed up and the light in the bathroom isn’t too harsh, I can think, well, maybe it’s not as bad right this second. I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trust, like your wife, were to tell you that you look fine and that your concerns about your appearance are unfounded, would that change how you feel about your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It might settle me for a minute, coming from her. She’ll say I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she means it kindly.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t really change it for long. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… I’ll be sitting with a banker or somebody from the county office, and I’m sure they’re looking right at all of it. Even if part of me can say maybe it’s just my perception, the bigger part of me doesn’t believe it for long.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your appearance ever completely go away, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe. I mean, my wife would say it’s exaggerated, I know that. She tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and around here that’s not exactly unusual. You go to the feed store and half the men have cracked hands and sun spots and all that.

But when it’s me, I can’t seem to see it that way. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. If I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel like they’re staring right at the cracks in my hands or the spots and sagging in my face. Maybe I’m making it bigger than it is, but in the moment it feels real.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is… I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—especially when I have to go into town or deal with bankers or county people, I feel like they’re all staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors his whole life, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face and hands, yeah. I mean, those are the parts people see first, especially when I’m sitting across from someone at the bank or talking to county folks about paperwork. I start thinking they’re looking at the cracks in my skin, the spots, the way my face seems to sag now.\n\nMy wife tells me it’s just what happens when a man’s worked outdoors his whole life, you know, sun and weather and machinery grease and all that, but… I don’t know. It doesn’t feel normal to me when I’m out around people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face and hands most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yeah… I guess so. I don’t always notice it right away, like if I’m out fixing something or dealing with the fields, but as soon as I’ve got to see people—bankers, county office folks, anybody like that—it gets in my head.\n\nI keep thinking about how rough my face looks, how worn my hands are, the cracks and spots and all that sagging. My wife says it’s just from working outside all these years, and maybe she’s right, but it’s hard for me to let it go. It kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the appearance of your face and hands interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. More than I like to admit. I’ve let calls from bankers go because I couldn’t get myself feeling like I looked decent enough to sit down and talk things through, even just on the phone knowing I’d have to meet them later. And then that holds up farm decisions, paperwork, things that ought to just get done.\n\nAnd with the retreat and the craft sales—my wife’s always saying a picture helps people feel welcome, or helps sell what we’re doing, and I know she’s right. But I’ve stopped agreeing to be in photos. I’ll find some excuse about needing to check a fence or move equipment, but really it’s because I don’t want my face or hands out there for people to look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands are actually abnormal in their appearance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “abnormal” is the right word… I mean, my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outside his whole life, and she’s probably trying to be kind, but when I look at myself, I can’t get past how rough and worn everything looks.\n\nMy face, my hands… the cracks, spots, the sagging. And then if I’m sitting across from a banker or somebody at the county office, I feel like that’s all they’re seeing. Like they’re staring at it, even if maybe they aren’t. I know plenty of men my age have weathered skin, but mine feels… worse somehow. Hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: When your wife or others tell you that you look fine, that your skin is just a result of working outdoors, do you think they're seeing the same things you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they are, no… or maybe they see it but it doesn’t hit them the same way. My wife will say, “That’s just sun and work,” like it’s nothing, like the old barn siding getting weathered—actually, we had boards on the south side go gray just like that after a few seasons—but with me, I can’t just see it as normal wear.\n\nWhen I’m looking at my face or my hands, all I can see are the cracks and spots and the sagging. And then if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I get it in my head that they’re noticing every bit of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the appearance of your face and hands stay the same all the time, or are there moments when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same every minute, no. If I’m out working and I’ve got gloves on, or I’m focused on some repair, I can forget it for a bit. Like when you’re fixing a gate hinge and the bolt’s stripped, you’re not thinking about much else.\n\nBut then if I have to go into town, or sit down with a banker or somebody from the county office, it comes right back. I start thinking they’re looking at my face, my hands, the cracks and spots and sagging. Even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how your face and hands look ever change, or is it always the same in your mind?\n\nPatient: It… it mostly stays the same in my mind, I guess. I mean, some days the light catches my face different or my hands are dirty from work, and I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just that, but I still come back to the same thing—that they look rough and worn out.\n\nIt’s the cracks and spots, the sagging, all of it. I’ll be sitting across from a banker or someone from the county office and I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe that’s true, but it doesn’t feel that simple to me. It’s like once I notice it, my mind just locks onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt your face and hands looked better, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe once in a while, if I’ve just washed up and the light in the bathroom isn’t too harsh, I can think, well, maybe it’s not as bad right this second. But it doesn’t last.\n\nI keep coming back to how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… that’s what brings me in, really. I’ll be sitting with a banker or someone from the county, and it’s like I can feel them looking at every bit of it, even though my wife keeps saying I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nI know farmers’ hands get beat up—my dad’s did too, and he used to say you could tell a man’s work by his hands—but with me, I can’t leave it at that. I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trust, like your wife, were to tell you that you look fine and that your concerns about your appearance are unfounded, would that change how you feel about your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It might settle me for a minute, coming from her. I do trust my wife—she’s known me since before my hands got this beat up, back when we were renting land and trying to make a go of it. She’ll say I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she means it kindly.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t really change it for long. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… I’ll be sitting with a banker or somebody from the county office, and I’m sure they’re looking right at all of it. Even if she tells me it’s unfounded, my mind doesn’t seem to take that in the way it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face and hands actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's distorted?\n\nPatient: I suppose there’s a possibility, yes… I mean, my wife says that all the time, that I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. And I’ve seen plenty of men my age at the co-op with hands worse than mine, I guess.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m sitting across from a banker or someone at the county office. I can’t get past it—how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it’s like I’m sure they’re looking right at it. Even if part of me can say maybe it’s just my perception, the bigger part of me doesn’t believe it for long.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your appearance ever completely go away, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. I might get distracted for a short spell—if I’m fixing something or talking about crop prices or, you know, trying to remember whether I shut a gate—but it comes back.\n\nWhat brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… especially if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county. I start thinking they’re staring right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. It just doesn’t really leave me alone.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular aspect of your face or hands that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pick just one. I start with my hands, usually—the cracks and the rough skin, like they’ve been through too many seasons. But then I’ll catch my face in a window or under those office lights and I see the spots and the sagging, and then it’s all I can think about.\n\nI guess what bothers me most is the whole worn-out look of my face and hands together. Like when I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel sure they’re staring at it, sizing me up, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. I know she means well, but I can’t seem to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face and hands look normal to other people, even if they don't to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I know that’s what my wife says. She’ll tell me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and there are plenty of fellows around here who look weathered, you know, especially after years in the sun and dirt.\n\nBut when I’m sitting across from a banker, or somebody at the county office, I can’t shake it. I keep thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging… like it’s all right there and they can’t help but notice. So yes, maybe it looks normal to them, but it sure doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the cracks, spots, or sagging that bothers you, or is it more the overall look of your face and hands?\n\nPatient: It’s… more the overall look, I think. The cracks in my hands get me started, and then the spots on my face, the sagging—well, it all piles together until I just look rough and worn out to myself.\n\nLike if I’m sitting with a banker or somebody from the county, I can’t help thinking they’re staring at all of it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I suppose that ought to settle it, but it doesn’t. I keep seeing it, the whole weathered look, and I can’t get past it.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your face and hands looking rough and worn ever change in intensity from day to day?\n\nPatient: It does change some, yes. Some days I can get on with things a little better, especially if I’m busy and not sitting under bright lights or across from somebody important.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I can’t really get past it overall—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I’ll be talking to a banker or someone from the county and I start thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s trying to help, but once I notice it, it sort of takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice the cracks, spots, and sagging on your face and hands, or is it just you who thinks they might be staring at them?\n\nPatient: I think they notice it. Or… I guess I don’t know for sure, but it feels like they do. Especially if I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county office, I get to thinking they’re looking right at my hands, the cracks, and then my face, the spots and the sagging.\n\nMy wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, same as plenty of men around here. And maybe she’s right. I mean, I’ve seen fellows at the feed store who look just as weathered, but somehow when it’s me, I can’t get past it. It feels like it’s written all over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face and hands because they are actually noticing something out of the ordinary, or because you feel self-conscious about how you look?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but it feels more like they’re noticing something. I mean, when I’m across from a banker or somebody from the county, I’ll see my hands on the table and the cracks look terrible to me, and then I start thinking they’ve seen them too. Then I’m thinking about the spots on my face, the sagging, all of it.\n\nMy wife says, “You just look like a man who’s worked outdoors,” and plenty of men around here do, you know—my neighbor’s hands are worse than mine sometimes, from fence work and cattle and all that. But when it’s me, I can’t let it go. It’s like I feel rough and worn before they even say anything, and then I read it into every look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face and hands might be exaggerated or not entirely accurate?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean, my wife would say it’s exaggerated, I know that. She tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and around here that’s not exactly unusual. You go to the feed store and half the men have cracked hands and sun spots and all that.\n\nBut when it’s me, I can’t seem to see it that way. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. If I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel like they’re staring right at the cracks in my hands or the spots and sagging in my face. Maybe I’m making it bigger than it is, but in the moment it feels real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And maybe she’s right. I mean, I’ve seen fellows at the feed store who look just as weathered, but somehow when it’s me, I can’t get past it. It feels like it’s written all over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face and hands because they are actually noticing something out of the ordinary, or because you feel self-conscious about how you look?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but it feels more like they’re noticing something. I mean, when I’m across from a banker or somebody from the county, I’ll see my hands on the table and the cracks look terrible to me, and then I start thinking they’ve seen them too. Then I’m thinking about the spots on my face, the sagging, all of it.\n\nMy wife says, “You just look like a man who’s worked outdoors,” and plenty of men around here do, you know—my neighbor’s hands are worse than mine sometimes, from fence work and cattle and all that. But when it’s me, I can’t let it go. It’s like I feel rough and worn before they even say anything, and then I read it into every look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face and hands might be exaggerated or not entirely accurate?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean, my wife would say it’s exaggerated, I know that. She tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and around here that’s not exactly unusual. You go to the feed store and half the men have cracked hands and sun spots and all that.\n\nBut when it’s me, I can’t seem to see it that way. What brings me in is I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. If I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I feel like they’re staring right at the cracks in my hands or the spots and sagging in my face. Maybe I’m making it bigger than it is, but in the moment it feels real."
  }
]
